<meta>This is the title page for the 1685 Leipzig edition of "Kern der Alchymie" (The Core of Alchemy). It establishes the work as a German translation of the writings of "Irenaeus Philalethes," a famous but mysterious figure in 17th-century alchemy. The layout follows the typical baroque style of presenting a detailed summary of the book's value directly on the cover.</meta>

# The Core
## Of Alchemy /
->That is<-
### A
# *TREATISE*
### proven through experience
->Which reveals<-
### the secret and highly hidden
### Mystery
->Of the<-
# Elixir of the Wise /
->Divided into two parts<-
## Whereof the first primarily
### explains the *Theory* <note>original: "Theoriam"</note> but the other the
### *Practice* <note>original: "Practicam"</note> of the Art /
### In which the Art is revealed so clearly /
### as has never happened before / for the benefit and
### service of young beginning workers / and for the conviction
### of those / who are entangled in the labyrinth of
### errors.
->Written<-
## By *IRENAEUS PHILOPONUS* <note>original: "Irenæum Philoponum" — A Greek-derived name meaning "Peaceful Lover of Labor."</note>
## *PHILALETHA.* <note>original: "Philaletham" — A pseudonym meaning "Lover of Truth." This identity was used by George Starkey, an American-born alchemist who became influential in the scientific circles of Robert Boyle and Isaac Newton.</note>
->Translated from the English<-
->by<-
## *Johann Lange.* <note>Lange was a prolific translator of medical and alchemical texts in the late 17th century.</note>

It introduces the famous pseudonym Irenaeus Philalethes to a German-speaking audience via translator Johann Lange.</summary>

# Preface
## By a Lover of Chemistry.
### Friendly Reader.

After the writings of the noble Englishman, the so-called Anonymous Philaletha <note>original: "Anonymi Philalethæ." This refers to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist whose identity was a closely guarded secret; modern scholarship often identifies him as George Starkey.</note>—specifically his *Open Entrance to the King's Closed Palace* <note>original: "Eröffneter Eingang zu des Königs verschloßnen Pallast," referring to the famous Latin work *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium*.</note> along with his other works—were made public through the printing press, many people have emerged who wished to explain and interpret this author’s obscure language. In this labor, one has always been more unsuccessful than the other, according to how each person’s mind was disposed. Among them all, however, none have come to my attention who have correctly grasped the author’s true meaning. Because much violence has been done to the truth by such misguided interpreters, and much harm has been caused to the beginning students of this hidden art <term>hidden art: Alchemy, the study of transmuting metals and seeking spiritual perfection</term>, I have often compassionately wished that an Adept <term>Adept: a master who has successfully achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy</term> might step forward to at least punish such "scribbling spirits" for their unfairness and warn the lovers of Chemistry against their interpretations. However, my

<summary>In this preface, an anonymous enthusiast of alchemy critiques the many poor interpretations of the works of Philalethes, a famous alchemical author. He expresses a desire for a true master (an Adept) to correct these errors and guide students away from false teachings.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, Eirenaeus Philalethes, Open Entrance to the King's Closed Palace, Adept, interpretation, chemical philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface from the previous page. The sentence begins with the completion of the word "Wunsch" (wish), following the catchword "Wüns" from the previous page.</meta>

) 0 (

...wishing was in vain for a long time, until I finally got this present treatise into my hands and read it. In this work, I have found that it not only agrees in all respects with the aforementioned Anonymous Author, but also fully explains that which was set down either imperfectly or unclearly in the *Open Entrance* <note>original: "Introitu aperto." This refers to Eirenaeus Philalethes' most famous work, *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium* (*Open Entrance to the King's Closed Palace*).</note>. Thus, this book can very well serve as an honest commentary <note>original: "Commentarii"</note> on the previously mentioned *Entrance to the King's Palace*. This is especially true since the author, having received the art <term>the art: Alchemy</term> from the very mouth of Philaletha, was best able to understand his meaning. 

I immediately applied myself so that it might be translated from English into German, and Mr. Johann Lange of Hamburg <note>Johann Lange (1629–1715) was a well-known physician and translator who brought many English alchemical and medical works to the German public.</note> willingly took this task upon himself. However, because of his other duties, he could not devote much time to it; therefore, a well-meaning reader will not judge too harshly if perhaps a word here or there was not translated with perfect precision. Indeed, I can assure you that, as far as the sense and meaning of the author are concerned, nothing has been changed in the slightest, much less over-refined; but due to great haste, the English style of speaking could not always be perfectly adapted to the German, especially since the English original was written in verse. 

Here I now present this work to you, and I wish that it may be received with as much good intention as...

it was made. Cardilucius <note>Johann Hiskia Cardilucius (1630–1697) was a physician and prolific editor of alchemical works, including those of Hartmann.</note> has already made mention of this treatise in his preface to the second part of his *Chemical Magnalia* <note>original: "Chymischen Magnalien"</note> and named George Starkey <note>original: "Georgium Stierkium"</note> as the author. However, he was mistaken in this, since Starkey only prepared the prefaces for it, as the anagrams *Excellent for Christ* <note>original: "Egregius Christo"</note> and *The Man is Guardian of the Flock* <note>original: "Vir Gregis Custos"</note> demonstrate. <note>Both Latin phrases are anagrams for "Georgius Stierkius." In the 17th century, authors often hid their identities or those of their associates using such wordplay.</note> The true name of the author is also known; but because he himself kept it secret, I have deemed it unnecessary to name him; especially since the name of a thing can neither give nor take anything away from it. It is only a pity that this beautiful treatise was not translated into German sooner, because without a doubt, a great light would have dawned for the seekers of the art <term>the art: Alchemy</term>, and many commentators would have been spared their vain labor. How much worry has arisen solely because of the two doves of Diana <note>original: "Tauben Dianæ." In the symbolic language of alchemy, the "Doves of Diana" often represent the white, volatile spirit or purified mercury used to "tame" the "philosophical dragon."</note>, and how many different interpretations have been made of them? Whoever wishes to take the trouble to read the writings available in public print regarding this will have to wonder that great people, who wish to be highly learned, can err so greatly. For instance, Dr. J. Becher <note>Johann Joachim Becher (1635–1682) was a major German scholar and alchemist whose work laid the foundations for the later phlogiston theory.</note> is much to be blamed in this regard, that in the second supplement of his *Physics* <note>original: "Physic," referring to his work *Physica Subterranea*.</note> he claims these doves to be two salts—namely, a urinous <term>urinous salt: an old term for ammonium salts or salts derived from animal matter</term> and an alkaline one—which are supposed to soften the arsenical sulfur in the regulus <term>regulus: the metallic mass that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting</term> and set the mercury <note>original: "Mercurium"</note> free; since such work,

even if it is practical in itself, it does not fit the opinion of the anonymous **Philaletha** <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes (the "Peaceful Lover of Truth") was the pseudonym of George Starkey, a highly influential 17th-century alchemist.</note> in the least, who says that one should temper the arsenical malignity <note>original: "Arsenicalische malignität." In alchemy, "malignity" refers to toxic or corrosive impurities that prevent the "noble" part of a mineral from being used.</note> in the **regulus** <term>regulus: The metallic heart of a mineral, usually antimony, obtained through smelting.</term> with the doves, so that **Mercury** <term>Mercury: In alchemy, this refers not just to the element, but to the "living" spirit or philosophical solvent necessary for the Work.</term> can mix with it; whereas on the other hand, the salts completely and utterly prevent all true ingress to the **Mercury**. Also, **Cardilucius** is not to be praised for leading the inexperienced astray in the preface to the three little treatises <note>original: "Tractätlein"</note> recently published under the name of **Philaletha**. In that he rejects those who take **Luna** <note>original: "Lunam." Latin for the Moon, signifying silver or the feminine principle in alchemy.</note> for the **doves of Diana** <note>original: "Columbis Dianæ." A symbolic name for a purified, volatile substance used to "tame" the metallic regulus.</note>, but proposes an even worse opinion in its place than the one he discards. He asks, and not unfairly, how **Luna** can be the two doves; but if he holds that **Luna**, insofar as she is only a single thing, cannot be these two doves, he gives sufficient understanding that he does not know the two doves found in the **forest of Diana** <note>original: "Walde Dianæ." A metaphorical term for the metallic kingdom or the starting material of the alchemical process.</note> (i.e., in the metallic kingdom) in a single **subject** <term>subject: The starting material or raw matter used in a chemical operation.</term>, much less does he remember that the poets assigned first this thing and then that to the gods, by whose names they afterwards frequently understood the thing itself designated by them. ...not [true], because otherwise he would act against the rule, *nature must be mixed with natures* <note>original: "natura naturis miscenda"</note>, which he so strongly recommends) [that they] would frequently, indeed almost always, be called "dead"; and what he cites in this regard from **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary figure in alchemy. Most works under this name in the 17th century were by a "Pseudo-Geber" whose chemistry was highly influential.</note> does not fit here at all, because **Geber** speaks of a far different sublimation of **Mercury** <term>Mercury: In this context, the "Philosophical Mercury," a vital solvent or spirit, rather than common quicksilver.</term> than our **Philaletha** does. 

It would be for the best if such writings, provided they have no surer foundation, were left out entirely; thus the truth would not be obscured, and the beginning students—who in any case prefer to read the German interpretations rather than the obscure [original] text—would not be so wretchedly misled. For it is not at all possible that one who fumbles in dark imaginings without experience could write a *Lamp of the Salt of the Philosophers* <note>original: "Lucernam Salis Philosophorum"</note>, or that he who is surrounded by the dark clouds of tangled errors could write a *Philosophical Clear Sky* <note>original: "Sudum Philosophicum"</note>, as the so-called **Son of Sendivogius** <note>original: "Filius Sendivogii." Michael Sendivogius was a famous Polish alchemist; this "Son" refers to an author writing under his lineage or pseudonym.</note> has done before. 

It is beyond all dispute that such writings cause the greatest contempt for the art of **Alchemy**, and the greatest harm to the untrained beginners. Therefore, every reasonable person should rightly observe the Golden Rule of Christ here: *what you do not want done to you, do not do to another* <note>original: "quod tibi non vis fieri, alteri ne feceris"</note>—what you want people to do for you, do also for them. For I do not believe that such an interpreter (who may at times write something out of a simple, good intention, since...

[while] others, on the other hand, do so out of deceitful hearts) would willingly allow themselves to be led onto wrong paths by an inexperienced person.

Indem meine Feder allhier auff dergleichen Materie gerathen / kan ich nicht umbhin auch Meldung zu thun von einem / dessen Nahme Joh. Otto de Helbig ist. Dieser ist bißher von vielen Unerfahrnen für einen warhafftigen Philosophum gehalten werden / wie er sich denn auch selbst dafür ausgegeben / und des Philosophischen Elixirs gerühmet / nebst einer andern neuen unerhörten Dingen mehr. Aber / leider! damit hat er sich verrathen / daß er unterschiedliche Scartelgen drucken lassen / in welchen er seine Meynung an Tag gegeben / aus welchen ein iedweder vernünfftiger Chymicus leicht urtheilen kan / wie weit sich seine Erfahrung in der Chymie erstrecket. Unter andern hat er ein Judicium über des edlen Anonymi Philalethæ Introitum ad Regis Palatium geschrieben / in welchem er Philaletham (dessen rechter Nahme seinen Lehr-Schülern gar wohl bekandt ist) gar sehr rühmet / und ihn verum Philaletham nennet / aus der Ursache / weil er mit ihme in allen überein komme. Allein wer beyder Schrifften gelesen / und dero Sinn verstehet / wird über dieses Fürgeben grausam lachen müssen. Der freundliche Leser wird mir nicht übel deuten / daß ich aus gedachtem Helbigischen Judicio einige Worte anher setze: **Mercury** of **Philaletha**, he says, arises not from **Antimony** or **Mars**, but from a certain airy matter, <note>original: "Oritur... Mercurius Philalethæ non ex Antimonio aut Marte, sed ex materia qvadam aërea." In alchemy, "Mars" is the symbolic name for Iron, and "Mercury" refers to the "Philosophical Mercury," the essential liquid principle of metals.</note>

As my pen has stumbled upon this subject here, I cannot help but mention someone whose name is **Johann Otto von Helbig** <note>Johann Otto von Helbig (1654–1698) was a German physician and alchemist who traveled to the East Indies and published several controversial alchemical works.</note>. Until now, he has been held by many inexperienced people to be a true **philosopher** <note>In the context of 17th-century alchemy, a "philosopher" refers to an adept who has mastered the secrets of nature and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, just as he has presented himself and boasted of the **Philosophical Elixir** <term>Philosophical Elixir: A substance capable of curing all diseases and transmuting base metals into gold.</term>, alongside other new and unheard-of things. But, alas! In this he has betrayed himself, for he has had various pamphlets <note>original: "Scartelgen," referring to small, often poorly made booklets or polemical tracts.</note> printed in which he has brought his opinions to light, from which any sensible **chemist** <note>original: "Chymicus"</note> can easily judge how far his experience in **chemistry** <note>original: "Chymie"</note> extends. Among other things, he has written a *Judgment* <note>original: "Judicium"</note> on the noble anonymous **Philaletha’s** *Entrance to the King's Palace* <note>original: "Introitus ad occlusum Regis Palatium," a highly influential alchemical work published in 1667, attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, in which he greatly praises **Philaletha** (whose true name is well known to his students) and calls him the *true Philaletha* <note>original: "verum Philaletham"</note> for the reason that he agrees with him in everything. However, anyone who has read both writings and understands their meaning must laugh cruelly at this pretense. The kind reader will not take it poorly if I set down a few words here from the aforementioned *Judgment* by Helbig: "The **Mercury** of **Philaletha**," he says, "arises not from **Antimony** or **Mars**, but from a certain airy matter," <note>original: "Oritur... Mercurius Philalethæ non ex Antimonio aut Marte, sed ex materia qvadam aërea."</note>

[airy] matter, <note>original: "aërea." This continues the sentence from the previous page.</note> which from the beginning of its preparation until the production of the fluid metal has never touched the earth upon which we walk; likewise, the <term>Mercury of the Philosophers</term> <note>The essential liquid principle or "soul" of metals in alchemy, not common quicksilver.</note> acknowledges more noble parents than <term>Antimony</term>, <term>Mars</term>, or <term>Luna</term>, <note>In alchemical nomenclature, Mars refers to Iron and Luna refers to Silver.</note> etc.

**That is:** The Mercury of Philalethes <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, the pseudonym for a famous 17th-century alchemist, author of *The Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King*.</note> does not come from **Antimony** or **Iron**, but from an **airy matter** which, from the beginning of its preparation until the final processing of the flowing metal, has never touched the earth on which we walk; likewise, the **Mercury of the Philosophers** has far more noble parents than **Antimony**, **Iron**, and **Silver**, etc.

Now, whoever has diligently read Philalethes’s *Entrance* will easily see how "excellently" Helbig has captured his meaning. For my part, I might almost be inclined to think that he has never even seen the inside of Philalethes’s book, were I not forced to consider it an irresponsible folly to pass judgment on something unknown. But be that as it may, Helbig exposes himself far too much through this, and by his untimely writing, he forces reasonable people to harbor great doubt regarding his philosophy. If I did not know that some inexperienced people still clung to him, I would not have bothered to mention him here. But because the **Hermetic Confederates** <note>original: "Confœderati Hermetici." Likely a specific circle of alchemical practitioners or students associated with Helbig.</note> especially suffer because of him—having publicly accepted him as their teacher—general charity has required me to do something in defense of the truth.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Johann Otto von Helbig’s misunderstanding of the alchemist Philalethes. The previous page ended with the author accusing Helbig of "irresponsible folly," and this page elaborates on Helbig's misinterpretation of Philalethes’s foundational principles regarding the nature of the Philosopher’s Stone.</meta>

For I might perhaps not be capable of attacking—and completely overthrowing—his new, unheard-of philosophy; therefore, it is only my intention to show how he has wronged the noble <term>Philalethes</term> <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, the pseudonym for a famous 17th-century alchemist, author of *The Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King*.</note> with his <term>Judgment</term> <note>Referring to Helbig's "Judicium," a critical commentary on Philalethes.</note> and attributed a completely false opinion to him, thereby also irresponsibly misleading his own followers.

May the lover of the Art <note>A traditional term for Alchemy.</note> be pleased to diligently read through one chapter after another in the <term>Open Entrance</term> <note>original: "Introitu aperto." This refers to Philalethes's primary text.</note>; he will find that Helbig has rejected exactly that which Philalethes has asserted. For our noble philosopher is not so foolish as to command that one should fish for the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> <note>original: "Goldmachenden Stein." The legendary substance said to transmute base metals into gold and grant long life.</note> out of the air, or seek it in non-metallic things outside of its own kind, because experience has taught him quite well that every thing is only capable of bringing forth its own kind. 

For this reason, right at the beginning of his book, he describes the Stone as being nothing other than gold, which through ingenious art is boiled to the highest degree of purity and penetrating subtlety, by means of such a <term>Moisture</term> <note>original: "Feuchtigkeit." In alchemical theory, this refers to a specialized solvent, often called the "mercury of the philosophers," which can dissolve gold without being corrosive.</note> which is entirely of one essence with the gold and differs from it only in quality. Since he then, under the name of gold, means nothing other than common, well-known gold (as he asserts in chapters 13 and 14), and under the moisture...

<summary>The author argues that Helbig has fundamentally misrepresented Philalethes by suggesting the Philosopher's Stone comes from "airy" or non-metallic sources. The author clarifies that Philalethes teaches the Stone is derived from refined gold and a specific metallic "moisture."</summary>
<keywords>Philalethes, Helbig, Philosopher's Stone, alchemy, gold, metallic essence, transmutation, The Open Entrance</keywords>

This page provides specific examples of how Helbig’s "magnet," "steel," and "mercury" differ fundamentally from the established definitions in Philalethes’s "Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King."</meta>

...but understands the running quicksilver, which has been animated with a pure metallic sulfur, as shines forth as clear as the sun from his writings. But Helbig has a completely different gold, and also a completely different <term>mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurium." In alchemical theory, this refers to the "sophic" mercury, the liquid principle or "water" of the metals.</note>; The thing that Philalethes calls his "magnet," he describes here and there, but particularly in the 11th Chapter, as follows: That it is a delicate metallic matter, which has been coagulated by a combustible sulfur within the ore-veins of the earth; also, that it is the well-known <term>Gold-bather</term> <note>original: "Goldbader." A traditional name for antimony, which was used in a molten "bath" to purify gold by consuming its impurities.</note> (*) which removes all impurities from common gold; but Helbig, despite his desire to agree with Philalethes in every respect, also has a different "magnet," which must be made from a human being, since, according to his claim, without the human being, or <note>original: "absq; animali." Latin for "without the animal [element].""</note>, the Stone can in no way be prepared. 

Likewise, when Philalethes calls the **philosophical steel** a metallic sulfur, a mineral fire, indeed in express words **Mars** <note>original: "Martem." In the symbolic language of alchemy, Mars is the code name for iron, which Philalethes used to "prime" his mercury.</note>, Helbig similarly has something else: namely, such a "steel" that must be caught from the air. And finally, he even has a completely different <term>Stone</term> <note>original: "Lapidem." The Philosopher's Stone or "Lapis Philosophorum."</note>, I believe, which must no doubt be far better than that of Philalethes, because he learned it from the **Indians and Arabs**, who, according to his claim, alone know the Art; whereas the poor Europeans (among whom Philalethes...

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s refutation of Johann Otto von Helbig. The previous page (ending with the catchword "but") discussed the differences between Philalethes’s "magnet" and "steel" and Helbig’s interpretations. This page focuses on the source of the Philosopher’s Stone, arguing that Philalethes intended for it to be derived from actual gold, not from the air or non-metallic substances as Helbig suggests.</meta>

For I might not be capable of attacking or completely overturning his new, unheard-of philosophy; therefore, my intention is only to show how he has done a disservice to the noble Philaletha <note>A reference to Eirenaeus Philalethes, a famous 17th-century alchemist whose identity remained a mystery for centuries.</note> with his judgment <note>original: "Judicio."</note> and attributed a completely false opinion to him, thereby also irresponsibly misleading his own followers <note>original: "Adhærenten."</note>.

Let the lover of the art <note>A common term for alchemy, the "Hermetic Art."</note> please read through one chapter after another in the *Open Entrance* <note>original: "Introitu aperto." Short for Philalethes’s primary work, *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium* (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note> with diligence; thus he will find that Helbig has rejected exactly what Philaletha has asserted. For our noble philosopher is not so foolish <note>original: "alber."</note> as to command one to fish the **Gold-making Stone** out of the air, or to seek it in non-metallic things outside of its own kind, because experience <note>original: "Experientz."</note> has taught him very well that every thing is only capable of bringing forth its own like. For this reason, he describes the Stone right at the beginning of his book, stating that it is nothing other than **gold**, which through ingenious art is boiled to the highest degree of purity and penetrating subtlety, by means of such a **moisture** <note>original: "Feuchtigkeit." In alchemical theory, this refers to the "Sophic Mercury," a liquid solvent that can dissolve gold into its prime matter without destroying its "seed."</note> which is entirely of one essence with the gold and differs from it only in quality. Since he, under the name of gold, [means] nothing other than common, well-known gold (as he asserts in chapters 13 and 14), and under the moisture...

I do not believe it would be poorly done if I were to now set down the opinion of **Philalethes** somewhat more fully, yet still briefly, so that when **Light and Darkness** are placed against one another, one may recognize each the more easily. Philalethes takes a **mercury** which was destined in the belly of the earth to become a metal, but was prevented by an accident so that it cannot be brought further by itself, unless its hidden **fermental sulfur** is awakened once again. He has learned that this sulfur is hidden very deeply in the **House of the Ram** <note>Aries, the first sign of the zodiac.</note> (wherein **Mars** <note>In alchemy, Mars is the code for the metal iron.</note> dwells)...

is hidden; this he draws out by means of **Vulcan’s service** <note>The "service of Vulcan" refers to the use of controlled fire or heat in the laboratory.</note> through **Saturnia** <note>original: "Saturniam." Slow heating in a sealed vessel.</note>, becomes the **medicine of the second order** / and then through **fermentation** and a new boiling becomes the **perfect elixir** / which can be multiplied at will **unto infinity** <note>original: "biß in infinitum."</note>.

This is, in brief, the natural process of Philalethes and all upright philosophers, which looks as much like Helbig’s process / as a wagon-load of hay looks like a roast pig; from which /

But this is not the opinion of Philalethes. I would also very much like to know the reason why Luna <note>original: "Luna." ### OF IRENAEUS PHILALETHA <note>original: "IRENAEI PHILALETHÆ." A famous pseudonym meaning "Peaceful Lover of Truth," attributed to the alchemist George Starkey.</note>
# The Core of Alchemy /
## First Part.
### Preface
### To the Kind Reader.

Kind reader / I wish to briefly inform you of what moved me to bring these hidden secrets of nature to the light of day / and to present to you the reasons for this, partly on behalf of the author and partly on my own. As far as the author is concerned / he was an eyewitness to the Great Secret <note>The "Great Secret" or *Magnum Opus* refers to the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold.</note> / as he himself testifies; and not only this / but through a gift he also possessed a portion of that same precious jewel <note>original: "Kleinod." Alchemical writers often referred to the Philosopher’s Stone as a jewel or treasure to emphasize its spiritual and physical value.</note> / which is sought by so many, but found by so few; which portion, although he [already possessed] it / in the hope [of...]

## Precept.

<margin>Note well.</margin>
I have found, taken into hand, purified, put together in the right weight, enclosed the **fruit of the womb** <note>In alchemical symbolism, the "fruit" or "child" refers to the emerging Philosopher’s Stone or the developing matter within the vessel.</note>, maintained in proper heat, waited, and seen these signs in their time: soon the woman has surrounded the man and divided his limbs from one another with a swift stroke; thus everything has turned to dust. After he has now been colored with a dark color on his divided limbs—

# The First Book.

It is a homogeneous <note>original: *Homogoneische*. This refers to a substance that is uniform in its composition, often called the "First Matter" (*Prima Materia*) by alchemists.</note> or uniform substance that lies before the eyes of the common man, from which mineral bodies are brought forth; for it is their sole true matter. This substance is altered through long digestion <note>In alchemy, "digestion" refers to the process of applying a gentle, constant heat over a long period—similar to how a body processes food—to mature and "ripen" the substance within a sealed vessel.</note> into many kinds, which differ from one another in their perfection. In like manner, there is an Art <note>Alchemy was frequently referred to simply as "The Art" (*Die Kunst*).</note> at which most people marvel, but few believe; few are those who know it. Through this Art, the metals that seek to flee from the fire <note>This refers to "volatile" metals or substances, such as mercury, which evaporate when heated.</note> are fixed <note>original: *figiret*. To "fix" a metal means to transform it from a volatile, evaporating state into a solid, stable state that can withstand high heat.</note> and so altered that they can withstand any blast of the bellows. This is the Transmutation, or the transformation (as the Sages say), of five metals into silver and six into perfect gold. It is a wonder that from base copper, tin, lead, or iron, in less than half an hour, perfect silver shall be made, which will nevermore be destroyed. So great is the virtue of this Divine Stone <note>Another name for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> that it not only does this, but also the most far-

it swells and sweats, and a smoke <note>original: *Rauch*. When this smoke has been condensed <note>original: *condensiret*. # The First Book.

...makes the most refined gold. Regarding this wondrous Art, whose power is so rare, the seeker <note>original: *Liebhaber*—literally a "lover" of the art, referring to a dedicated student or devotee of alchemy.</note> will no doubt wish to hear more; yet not everyone who cherishes it can attain this science or possess the skill to transform the perfected metals from their state of corruption and rescue the "sick" <note>In the alchemical worldview, base metals like lead or iron were considered "diseased" or "immature" versions of gold. Transmutation was viewed as a process of "healing" or "perfecting" the metal.</note> from the danger of destruction.

Since not everyone possesses the right or calling for this Art—but only those whom God in Heaven has chosen and appointed as its heirs—the rest may grope for want of light until they lose themselves in the dark. For this reason, such people mock even the most learned men and bitterly rail against all who follow them. Nevertheless, know above all else that this Art is certainly true, and its foundations are rooted in Nature itself. And though its detractors are many, and few can be found who dare to stand in its defense, this science shall—notwithstanding all this—remain forever unshakeable; it is a truth that no mockery can stain or defile. Nor shall the truth be overturned by the fact that, from time to time, certain impostors

<meta>This page continues the author’s philosophical defense of alchemy, focusing on the credibility of witnesses and the necessity of social trust. But it is not reasonable to judge those who present the Art <term>Art (*Kunst*): In this context, the "Great Art" of alchemy, encompassing the study of nature's secrets and the transformation of matter.</term> as truth to be false, and to immediately consider them as impostors. For if this rule were to hold, not a single thing would be found that could find belief, unless we knew it for ourselves; and such a thing would bring the whole world into confusion. From such a root, the absurd consequence would grow that those who find it so difficult to believe might easily consider everything to be wrong. 

It seems to me that if I were to read or hear of something held to be true from someone against whom I had no legitimate objection <note>original: *Exception*—a legal term referring to a formal challenge against the validity of a witness or their testimony.</note>, it would be a shame for me to criticize the truth of what I heard; for why should I not trust others, if I myself wish to be believed by them? Do unto others as you would have done to yourself in that which is right. 

But even more than this: we do not have here merely two or three witnesses, blameless people whose words we might reasonably believe; rather, this matter is confirmed by the pens of many hundreds, and is daily being even more confir- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword *bestä-*, likely continuing as *bestätigt* (confirmed) on the next page.</note>

All eras, lands, and peoples bring us a mountain of testimony from such distinguished men—regarding both their experience and scholarship—so that their mere words seem powerful enough to confirm this truth. If, therefore, the words and oaths of honest people—and indeed of those who were very famous and held an honorable reputation while they lived, and even afterward, at the very moment they were about to die—if these (I say) should prove nothing, then I would not know what to think. <meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, shifting from historical anecdotes (like those of Van Helmont and de Boodt) to a logical and philosophical defense of alchemy. The author aims to prove that the "Art" is grounded in the laws of Nature as established by God. The catchword from the previous page was "possible," leading into the opening phrase "to serve."</meta>

...to serve [the reader], to prove and demonstrate through constant logical deductions <note>original: *Schluß-Reden*; refers to syllogisms or formal arguments</note> and reasons that the Art <term>the Art: Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art" or the "Great Art" by its practitioners</term> is nevertheless possible. Even if many go astray in their research of it, this does not harm the truth; let those who begin the Work <term>the Work: the *Magnum Opus* or the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone</term> take care how they go about their tasks, if they ever intend to gain anything therein.

As for the first principle, I hope you will understand the nature of that which we wish to find; for otherwise you would be unfit to take such things in hand if you did not know how like produces its like. God has prescribed this to Nature as a law: that every thing shall take its form from its own kind. He has commanded that all things shall grow and multiply—all herbs together with the trees, all birds and all manner of species of animals, likewise the fish and humans—who understand what Reason <note>original: *Vernunfft*</note> has given them cause to do. However, each must grow within its own kind through propagation, which shall never cease. To continue this propagation, He has [endowed] every thing with its seed—

<meta>This page marks the beginning of "The Second Book." It follows the conclusion of the first book, where the author established the theoretical foundations of the "seminal power" and "Central Fire." Having defended alchemy's theoretical possibility in the previous section, the author now shifts his focus toward providing empirical evidence and eyewitness testimony to silence critics.</meta>

# The Second Book.

We have undertaken to rescue the noble art of **Alchemy** and, with logical arguments <term>logical arguments: structured reasoning or syllogisms used in formal scholastic debate</term> <note>original: *Schluß-Reden*</note>, have defied the most arrogant enemy who continues to slander it. For this reason, we have exerted all our strength through certain foundational proofs to demonstrate its possibility. 

The witnesses of those who were **artists** <term>artist: in this context, a "Philosopher by Fire" or a practicing alchemist who has mastered the Work</term> <note>original: *Künstler*</note> themselves are almost countless; and no less numerous are those who, although unable to attain it through their own **experience** <term>experience: often used in early modern texts to mean "experimentation" or practical trial</term> <note>original: *Erfahrung*</note>, have nevertheless been convinced by visual demonstration that it is true, and not—as some hasty judges believe—a mere fantasy held without foundation. 

And because that **basis of proof** <note>original: *Beweiß-Grund*</note> is most reliable which is presented for trial through experience, I can likewise prove the truth through the very same; for these are not merely idle thoughts, but I have visual testimony, the denial of which would only befit an irrational person. I know an artist, with

# Reflection
<note>original: *Nachsinnen*</note>

...that every thing has its own **disposition**, and every thing is guided according to its nature and constitution. I found my **medicine** <term>medicine: often called an "arcanum" or "arkney," this refers to the refined alchemical substance capable of healing metals or the human body</term> <note>original: *Arkney*</note>, which in its whiteness is like **dust motes in a sunbeam** <note>original: *Sonnenstäublein*; this describes the extremely subtle, shimmering powder of the perfected stone</note>, just as the **artist** <term>artist: the alchemist, who uses "art" to assist and perfect the natural processes</term> finds it after **nature** has formed itself, once a bright luster has been brought forth out of the darkness <note>This refers to the transition from the black stage (*Nigredo*) to the white stage (*Albedo*).</note>. Then, whoever intends to increase it in **virtue** <term>virtue: the inherent medicinal power or "strength" of the substance</term> <note>original: *Tugend*</note> or quantity must arrange his work accordingly. 

If his intention is to increase its weight, he may **imbibe** <term>imbibe: to soak or saturate a dry substance with liquid until it has "drunk" its fill</term> it—while it is not yet cold—with warmed **milk** <note>In alchemy, "milk" (such as Virgin's Milk) is a metaphorical term for a specific mercurial solvent used to "nourish" the growing Stone.</note>, then seal the glass and watch his fire well. Then he may be certain—yet not so bold as to give it such an excess of "milk" that it becomes oversaturated, nor should he then forget to give it its food. 

But if his glass has once become cold, he must truly **ferment** <term>fermentation: a stage where the Stone is combined with a "ferment" (like pure silver for the White Work) to fix its power and allow it to act on other metals</term> his **white work** <term>white work: the Albedo stage, the second major goal of the Great Work, which results in a Stone that can transmute base metals into silver</term> and observe the proper **proportion**. For when one ferments, he may make the **compound** <note>original: *Compositum* (Latin); the chemical mixture or union of substances.</note> too moist or too dry, which requires a carefulness that a stranger or an ignorant person will not bother with.

<summary>The author describes the delicate transition from the "black" stage to the "white" stage of alchemy, explaining how to "feed" the substance with "milk" to increase its weight. He emphasizes that the fermentation of the White Work requires precise proportions and heat management to avoid ruining the compound.</summary>

... leftover of the **white Medicine** <note>In alchemy, the "White Medicine" or "White Stone" refers to the stage of the Great Work capable of transmuting base metals into silver, preceding the final Red Stone.</note>, I preserved it with the intent that, with God's help, I would not rob myself during my life of the entire **substance** of such a great **treasure** or **secret**, but rather to keep it for the sake of Him who had given it to me.

# The Third Book.

Now I begin to describe the wondrous history of the **golden stream** <term>original: "güldener Fluss"; a metaphorical term for the "tincture" or the flow of the Philosopher's Stone that "streams" through and transforms base metals.</term>. This is the **scythe** / which, with a single stroke, cuts down all **desires** and **greed** / as the root of all evil. Whoever possesses this / does not fear the reversals of **Fortune**; he throws temporal things at his feet; His work is solely and alone to behold and contemplate his **God**; he regards silver / precious stones / and gold as earth and filth. This is the **Tree of Life** / which [the]

# The Fourth Book.

# The Fourth Book.

# The Fourth Book.

Thus far we have praised Alchemy <term>Alchemy</term> <note>original: "Alchymie".</note> according to its merits, and the subject <term>subject</term> <note>original: "Subjectum"; a Latin term referring here to the fundamental material or the underlying basis of the alchemical operation.</note> seems well worth such renown; for it is so noble and incomparable that many rightly prize it as divine. Now, however, it remains to be explained how and by what means such a thing is to be attained. For many who have sought it with great toil and suffering, and with the expenditure of exceedingly high costs, have gained nothing but beggary and poverty through the loss of their money and time, and have never arrived at this science <term>science</term> <note>original: "Wissenschafft"; in this period, the term referred to a systematic body of hidden or occult knowledge rather than modern empirical science.</note>. Since then fate or misfortune has afflicted so many, most people believe that the Art <note>original: "die Kunst"; the standard historical term for the practice of alchemy.</note> is either a fabrication or does not exist at all; who has not heard the many sorrowful complaints of those who have been brought to poverty by this search and have lived out their lives in ruin? Who has heard of anyone who increased his wealth through this science? Therefore they say this thing is but a fable <note>original: "Fabel".</note> or a fairy tale <note>original: "Mährlein".</note>, which the artists <term>artists</term> <note>original: "Artisten"; referring to alchemists or practitioners of the chemical arts.</note> are not able to make true

<summary>This page introduces the Fourth Book by contrasting the divine reputation of alchemy with the common reality of failed practitioners who end in poverty. It addresses the growing skepticism of the public, who often view alchemy as a mere fairy tale due to these frequent failures.</summary>

<keywords>Alchemy, The Art, alchemical failure, poverty, Artisten, science of alchemy, skepticism</keywords>

...be able. I do not wish at this time to touch upon all the various operations, nor to venture to show the types and manners of every error, because the reader would find little benefit in it—preferring, as he does, to continue learning the laws of Nature rather than taking the trouble to hear a recital of the sophistries <term>sophistries</term> <note>original: "Sophistereyen"; fallacious or deceptive arguments used to mislead seekers.</note> and deceptions of all the erring societies.

However, I will occasionally mention the errors of those who deviate from the right path—those who melt Nature together like a lump of wax into strange, monstrous creatures. When such people test their work through trial, they find it to be outside of its proper genus <note>original: "Geschlechte"; meaning the resulting substance is not of the correct nature or species intended by the Art.</note> and all in vain.

The first matter <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "erste Materie"; the fundamental starting material of the alchemical "Great Work."</note> that we take for our work is gold alone, and alongside it Mercury <term>Mercury</term> <note>original: "Mercurius"; in alchemy, this often refers to "philosophical mercury" rather than common quicksilver.</note>. We cook these together for so long that neither wishes to leave the other; in this work, they both die and rot through putrefaction <term>putrefaction</term> <note>original: "Putrefaction"; a stage of the alchemical process where the matter decomposes and turns black, signifying the "death" of the old form before a new one can arise.</note>. After this, they are both reborn in glory <note>original: "Herrligkeit".</note>. But we do not mean—as fools interpret our words—by "gold" some sort of insignificant substance; for many people use such names...

<meta>Continuing from the title page of the second part, this page serves as a preface to the reader. <image-desc>A decorative rectangular headpiece composed of sixteen repeating floral woodcut blocks arranged in two rows of eight. Each block depicts a stylized flower with leaves.</image-desc>

### A Reminder
## To the Kind and Art-Diligent Reader.

Kind Reader. In the previous part, you had the theory <note>original Latin: "Theoriam"</note>; in this part, however, the practice <note>original Latin: "Practica"</note> is explained. Use it well, and you shall be freed from all the snares and deceptions of the false alchemists. At first, I was not inclined to add anything further to this little treatise <note>original: "Tractätlein"</note>, considering that it is capable enough on its own to prove the truth of this Art <note>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the Great Work of transmuting base metals into gold.</note>. But because I know that many people make it their great concern to oppose the true light by employing deception for their own advantage, I wish to trouble you with a few lines, which perhaps will not be troublesome to you—

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces the practical volume of his work, positioning it as a safeguard against the fraudulent "false alchemists" who deceive the public. He asserts that while the text is self-sufficient, these introductory remarks are necessary to guide the reader toward the "true light" of chemical practice.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Practice vs Theory, False Alchemists, The Art, Hermetic Guidance, Eireneus Philalethes</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>Continuing from the preface on page 102, the author concludes his opening thought regarding the reader's diligent application of alchemical practice before outlining the three main goals of this specific treatise.</meta>

...will be [certain], if you employ them with earnestness. The purpose of this **little treatise** <note>original: "Tractätlein"</note>, as you shall find for yourself in reading, is to demonstrate that the **Art of Alchemy** is true, neither fictional nor false, as some judge who claim it is merely an old wives' tale or a work of fables.

This demonstration is achieved first through the cited testimony of such people who were themselves **Adepts** <term>Adepti <note>In alchemy, an Adept is someone who has successfully discovered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone and mastered the transformation of base metals into gold.</note></term> of their Art, and also of those who made no professional claim to the Art. Thus, an Art which has been confirmed by the testimony of its own **"Sons"** <note>Alchemists often referred to themselves and their colleagues as "Sons of the Art" or "Sons of Hermes," implying a spiritual and intellectual lineage.</note> as well as by strangers—all of whom were credible men—cannot be brought into dispute or doubt, except by irrational mockers.

Next, the author adds powerful **arguments** <note>original: "Schluß-Reden," referring to logical conclusions or formal discourses.</note> which prove the credibility and possibility of that which the **Artists** <note>In the 17th century, "Artist" (Artistinnen/Artisten) was the standard term for a skilled practitioner of a laboratory craft, specifically alchemy.</note> promise through their Art. 

Thirdly, he provides his own testimony based on experience, concerning that which he has seen with his own eyes, touched with his own hands, and specifically **handled** <note>original: "tractiret," from the Latin "tractare," meaning to process or work materials in a laboratory setting.</note>; upon which ground he, as an eyewitness...

<summary>This page outlines the author's three-fold argument for the validity of alchemy: the testimony of established Adepts, logical proofs of its possibility, and the author's own firsthand experimental evidence. He dismisses critics of the "Art" as irrational mockers who ignore the testimony of credible witnesses.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Adepts, Proof of Alchemy, Sons of Art, Experimental Evidence, Alchemical Witnesses, 17th-century Science</keywords>

In the second part, which is this one, he clearly reveals the **Practice** <term>Practica <note>The actual physical labor and experimental operations performed at the furnace.</note></term>, though in such a manner that only the **Sons of this Art** <note>A traditional term for initiated alchemists who have inherited the secret wisdom of the "hermetic" lineage.</note> will understand it, while remaining obscure enough for the **Sophists** <term>Sophiſten <note>In alchemical literature, this refers to false practitioners or pretenders who use logic to deceive rather than following the true laws of nature.</note></term>.

We begin with **Artephius** <note>A legendary alchemist, likely writing in the 12th century, who was famously said to have lived for over a thousand years through his alchemical secrets.</note>, the incomparable philosopher. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Secret Fire" or alchemist's solvent, moving from the testimony of Artephius to his commentator Johannes Pontanus. <page-num>106</page-num>

Pontanus erred two hundred times in the **Practice** <note>referring to the *Practica*, the practical application of alchemical theory</note>, but only in the discovery of the single **Agent** <note>original: "Agentis." The active substance or catalyst that triggers the transformation.</note> in the world, through which one can make the moisture of the **perfect body** <note>In alchemy, gold is considered the only "perfect" body because it does not rust or decay.</note> return, and reveal that which is hidden, and make it raw again and <term>re-incrudate <note>To return a substance to its raw or "crude" state (from the Latin *reincrudatio*) so it can be transformed again.</note></term> it—to use the words of **Artephius**. 

This **antimonial vinegar** of Artephius, this **mineral fire** of Pontanus—which are equivalent terms—is the foremost and greatest secret, which in truth is the **Moon**, which is no metal, even though it is already the Sun or Gold. Therefore, although he speaks of two bodies, namely of the Sun and the Moon, it is nevertheless to be understood that the Sun alone is present. 

It is indeed true that in the <term>fermentation <note>The final stage of the work where a "starter" of pure silver or gold is added to the alchemical medicine to determine its final effect.</note></term> <margin>T</margin> true silver is used for the **White Work** <note>The second stage of the Great Work, which produces the ability to transmute base metals into silver.</note>, just as gold is for the **Red Work**; but not in the **First Work**. For first, the Sun is dissolved in the water by means of the Moon, which is a tender, bright, and pure body, according to the teaching of **Ripley** <note>Sir George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist and canon of Bridlington whose writings were highly influential in early modern Europe.</note>.

<summary>This page explains that the core secret of alchemy is a specific "Agent" or "Mineral Fire" (often called Antimonial Vinegar) used to dissolve gold back into its raw state. It clarifies that while silver and gold are used in the final fermentation, the initial dissolution requires a specific "Moon" that is not the common metal.</summary>

There are some who are very cunning and crafty in their interpretation of **Gold** in **Philosophy** <note>In this context, "Philosophy" refers to Alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Philosophy."</note>. Such people are certain **Alchemists** whose brains our **Moon** <note>A play on words: the "Moon" (Luna) refers both to the alchemical solvent discussed on the previous page and to the source of "lunacy" or madness.</note> has confused all too much, so that they foolishly take for **Gold** that which a modest man would find burdensome even to accept as **Silver**. # The Core of Alchemy / Second Part.

-> * * * <-

### The First Book.

We have both proven the golden art—which is so highly esteemed by many—and taught through examples that it is not a fable, as many imagine, but is true and exists in reality. Now we also wish to explain the practical application <note>original: "Practicam"</note>, through which silver and gold can be obtained.

Therefore, consider as the foundation of that which we aim for, and weigh with good understanding the causes of our Work <note>The "Work" (original: "Werck") refers to the *Magnum Opus* or Great Work—the process of transmuting base metals into gold</note>; otherwise you might apply your expenses in vain, and your Work might not repay the useless costs you spend upon it, in that you therefrom...

<summary>This page introduces the second part of the treatise, transitioning from a theoretical defense of alchemy to the "Practica," or the actual laboratory methods for producing silver and gold.</summary>
<keywords>golden art, alchemy, practical work, silver, gold, Magnum Opus, laboratory practice</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Practica" from the previous page. The text picks up mid-sentence, describing the failure of incorrect alchemical methods before defining the true nature of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

...would collect nothing but stench and smoke. The **Stone** which you seek is—as we have always said and confirmed—nothing other than **gold**, which has been brought to as high a perfection as is humanly possible. And although it is such a very closed and compact body, yet through the governance of **Art** <note>In alchemy, "Art" refers to the skill and laboratory techniques of the alchemist, as opposed to processes that occur in nature without human help.</note> and the cooperation of **Nature**, it becomes a **tincturing spirit** <term>tincturing spirit: a spiritualized, concentrated form of the metal capable of "tingeing" or transforming the properties of other substances.</term>, which remains forever and is imperishable. 

This Stone cannot be made through the diligent action of Nature alone, because her intention is not to bring gold to such a high state; instead, whoever wishes to obtain this **essence** must ensure that the gold is first softened and dissolved into a dust, and afterwards into a mineral water. When this has been **circulated** <note>Circulation is a process where a liquid is heated in a closed vessel so that the vapors rise, condense, and fall back down in a continuous cycle.</note> with the proper fire, until all moisture is consumed and **fixed** <note>original: "figirt"; in alchemy, to "fix" a substance means to make it solid and stable so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to heat.</note> by dryness, it must then be frequently **imbibed** <note>Imbibition involves gradually adding liquid to a dry substance until it is saturated.</note>, **coagulated** again, and enclosed like a child in its mother’s womb; there it is nourished for so long until it reaches its proper strength, so that it will be able to overcome all powerful opposition. Thereafter, ferment...

For if you either lose your costs and time, and gather nothing but loss and damage, surely you would not want to be regarded as someone who fell into **poverty** through this Art? Or if you won the incomparable **prize**, who would want to be known as one who has the **Art of making gold**? It is better to enjoy such things in silence than to boast of them in the public street, and thus foolishly subject yourselves to those who desire to ruin you because of the Art, if they do not [receive] the same from you—

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the Third and Final Book of the manuscript. Following the previous page's introduction to the difficult "blackness" and "rotting" stages of the work, the author now shifts to a systematic explanation of the individual operations of alchemy, beginning with Calcination.</meta>

# The Third and Final Book.

In the preceding books, the art of **alchemy** <term>Alchemy (*Alchymie*): The ancient spiritual and chemical practice aimed at purifying and perfecting matter, often associated with turning base metals into gold or attaining spiritual enlightenment.</term> has been described. Now it remains specifically to discuss how the same is divided into different parts, and to explain each and every one of them. This final book teaches this. Therefore, direct your mind with earnest reflection upon the truth that you can find herein.

## Calcination.

First, we **calcine** <term>Calcination (*Calcinatio*): A process of heating a solid substance to a high temperature in a crucible to achieve decomposition or to make it brittle and powdery. Alchemically, it is the first step in "opening" a metal to extract its inner spirit.</term> and bring the body to **porosity** <term>Porosity (*Porosität*): The state of being full of tiny pores or holes; here, it refers to making the dense metal permeable so that the "life-giving spirit" can be accessed.</term>, otherwise we may in no way obtain the life-giving life which makes every single thing alive. The body appears dead when this life has departed from it. This alone is what we desire, and through clever art we find it in **Hermes’ vessel** <note>original: *Hermetis Gefäß*; the sealed glass flask or "Philosopher's Egg" used for alchemical transformations, named after the legendary founder of alchemy, Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. This is the **mortification of the spirit** <note>original: *Tödtung des Geistes*; the symbolic "death" or breakdown of the material's initial form, allowing for its subsequent purification and rebirth.</note> when the earth is made loose

<summary>This page introduces the final book of the manuscript, which focuses on explaining the specific operations of alchemy, starting with Calcination. It describes the necessity of breaking down the "dead" body of the metal to reach the life-giving spirit hidden within.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, calcination, porosity, Hermes' vessel, mortification of the spirit, purification, alchemical operations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Calcination from page 161. We leave this path to the fools, for it serves nothing but the loss of the property of those who follow it; but you, beware of it. 

For we calcine in its own kind alone, by mixing the raw with that which is entirely perfect. The first dissolves the fixed, and the other **fixes** <note>In alchemy, to "fix" a substance is to make it non-volatile, so it can withstand heat without evaporating.</note> that which is volatile. The work of those who take such waters that wet the hands is lost, for such waters do not serve our Art. <note>original: *Wasser... welche die Hände naß machen*; the author is distinguishing between common liquids (like acids or water) and the "Philosophical Mercury," which was famously described as a "water that does not wet the hands."</note> Such people build upon sand.

<meta>This page completes a sentence begun on page 162, describing the substance as "a mother, bride, and sister" to the metallic matter. It continues the discussion on the "Secret Fire" and the unique "Salt of Nature" required for true alchemical transformation, contrasting the work of true philosophers with that of "sophists" or false alchemists.</meta>

...a mother, bride, and sister, and agrees with it in quality. There is no other active **agent** <term>Agent (*Agens*): The active substance or force that causes a chemical or spiritual transformation in alchemical processes.</term> that can be compared to this; all others lead astray. This is the **salt of nature** which we keep hidden; if the Almighty had not created it, the art of alchemy would be in vain. For by its power, gold is made raw again; it adds to its weight and holds within itself a secret fire which causes the gold to putrefy <note>original: *faulen*; in alchemy, putrefaction is the essential stage where the substance breaks down and blackens before being reborn.</note>. is subjected. So you now know, as I trust, what we understand by the **dissolution** <term>Dissolution: In alchemy, the stage where a solid substance is broken down into its liquid or essential form.</term>.

## Separatio <note>original: *Separatio*; Latin for Separation.</note>

Now I come to **separation** or "cleaving" <note>original: *Scheidung*; the act of dividing or sorting different substances from a mixture.</note> to treat of it, which has such a great affinity with the previous operation that both meet in one another and proceed on the very same path. Only in their **motion** <note>original: *Motion*; the physical movement or activity of the substances within the vessel.</note> or movement are they distinguished; otherwise, they are entirely and completely united with one another. This **dissolution** <term>Dissolution: The process of breaking down a solid substance into a liquid or essential form.</term> does not pass through the entire **body** <note>original: *Leib*; the material substance being worked upon in the vessel.</note>; rather, it still retains something of its **corporality** <note>original: *Corporalität*; its physical density or bodily nature.</note>, and has become a third thing—neither entirely **volatile** <term>Volatile: A substance that easily evaporates; in alchemy, this represents the "spiritual" aspect of the matter.</term> nor entirely **fixed** <term>Fixed: A substance that remains solid and stable even when subjected to high heat.</term>, yet neither of the two. ## Separation <note>original: *Separatio*</note>

Now I come to **separation** <note>original: *Scheidung*</note> or dividing, to treat of it, which has such a great affinity with the previous operation that both meet in one another and proceed along the same path. But first of all, the **waters** <note>The liquid essences within the flask.</note> are like a flame, rising up like a **vapor or mist**. For all our art is merely to open and unlock what is closed; and afterwards to **coagulate** <term>Coagulate: To thicken or solidify a liquid; in alchemy, this represents the grounding of spiritual forces into a physical form.</term> <note>original: *coaguliren*</note> it again; to make it volatile, and then to **fix** <term>Fix: To stabilize a substance so it no longer evaporates when heated.</term> <note>original: *figiren*</note> it again; to bring it to death, and afterwards to make it alive again and to heal it; to **putrefy** <term>Putrefy: The stage of decay where the substance turns black, signaling the death of the old form before rebirth.</term> <note>original: *putrificiren*</note> it, and to cleanse it again. <meta>This page continues from page 190, completing the hyphenated term "gold-making powder." The author is beginning his theoretical defense of the composition of the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

-making powder is nothing other than gold, which through ingenious art has been refined to the highest degree of the most perfect purity and the most subtle <term>fixity</term> <note>original: Fixität. In alchemical theory, "fixity" refers to a substance's ability to withstand the heat of fire without evaporating or changing.</note>, as the excellent Philaletha <note>Eirenaeus Philalethes, a 17th-century alchemist whose influential writings were widely studied by figures like Isaac Newton.</note> says right at the beginning of his *Open Entrance* <note>original: introitus aperti. Referring to the famous 1667 treatise *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium* (An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King).</note>. From this it follows that the very foundation of the gold-making art is gold itself, according to the common rule: Like produces like <note>original: Omne simile producit suum simile. This was a foundational principle of medieval and early modern science.</note>. Since the <term>Philosopher’s Stone</term> is primarily meant to possess the quality of transforming imperfect metals into good gold, it must necessarily consist of gold itself, for it is impossible for someone to give what they do not possess. I could support this thesis with many reasons <note>original: rationibus.</note>, but because reason <note>original: ratio.</note> alone is not sufficient here—since a person can use beautiful logical arguments to dress up something that does not hold true in practice <note>original: Experientz. Here meaning experimental or practical experience.</note>—I turn instead to the authority of the Philosophers <note>original: ad Authoritatem Philosophorum.</note>, whom one ought to trust as honest men in this matter. The authors whom I consider authentic are Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the mythical father of the "Hermetic" arts.</note>, Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath and foundational figure in chemistry.</note>, Artephius <note>A medieval alchemist to whom legendary longevity was attributed.</note>, Arnoldus <note>Arnold of Villanova, a prominent 13th-century physician and alchemical writer.</note>,

<summary>The author argues that the Philosopher's Stone must be made of gold because a substance cannot impart a quality (like "goldness") that it does not already possess. He appeals to the authority of famous historical alchemists rather than relying solely on logic.</summary>

Now that I have explained both the **matter of the Stone** <note>original: "materia Lapidis." The raw materials required to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> and the **mercury**, I finally turn to the practical work, which I shall present as clearly as possible.

First of all, before one combines the **gold** with its water, it must be <term>calcined</term> <note>Reduced to a fine powder through high heat or chemical corrosion.</note> in the subtlest manner, so that its particles are even much smaller than **motes of dust in the sun** <note>original: "Sonnenstäublein." A poetic description for a powder so fine that it behaves like dust floating in a sunbeam.</note>. For otherwise, it would resist the <term>solution</term> <note>The process of dissolving the solid metal into a liquid state.</note>, and the work would become very tedious or even fail entirely.

After this, place it in a glass <term>retort</term> <note>A spherical glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used in the distillation of volatile substances.</note> and distill the **mercury** from it into a receiver full of cold water; in this way, the **Doves of Diana** <note>A symbolic name for silver that has been purified through antimony. The "doves" refer to the white, shimmering appearance of the metal or the crystalline "feathers" that form during the process.</note> will remain behind dead—which must also be dead at the beginning if they are to be of any use. Repeat this labor seven to ten times, and thus the wise creation of the **mercury** is completed.

In this labor, the **Mercury of the Sages** is extracted; however, the water—namely the **common quicksilver**—is improved by the addition of the **true sulfur**, which is contained within the **Mercury of the Regulus** <note>The "Regulus" is the metallic heart of antimony. Its "sulfur" is the active, combustible principle that "perfects" or enlivens the liquid mercury.</note>, so that it may be entirely suitable for the **Bath of the King** <note>A specialized solvent, often a mercurial preparation, used to dissolve gold (the "King") to begin the process of transmutation.</note>. Thus, the secret of the **Mercury of the Sages** is quite clearly revealed and explained. Regarding the **Doves of Diana**, there might still be some difficulty, as they are not named so clearly and precisely. But whoever diligently reads and understands **Philalethes** <note>Referring to Eirenaeus Philalethes, the pseudonym of a 17th-century alchemist whose works, like "The Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King," were foundational to later laboratory work.</note> and **Jean d'Espagnet** <note>A French alchemist (1564–1637) whose work "Enchiridion Physicae Restitutae" was a standard text for understanding the "Secret of the Stone."</note> will find that they cannot remain hidden, especially if he considers what properties...

...they should be. The reason why the **doves** must be used for this is because the **regulus** <note>The "regulus" refers to the metallic heart of antimony, considered a "royal" metal in alchemy.</note> by itself cannot be mixed with **common mercury** <note>original: "Mercurio vulgi"</note> due to the **arsenical sulfur** that clings to it and binds its fluidity.

Yet I cannot neglect to warn my Lord to beware of lead, tin, bismuth, and the like, which, as **Geber** <note>Abu Musa Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath and "father of chemistry" whose works were central to European alchemy.</note> testifies, contaminate the **mercury** with a foul blackness.

Now that I have explained both the **matter of the Stone** <note>original: "materiam Lapidis," referring to the physical starting material for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> and the **mercury**, I finally turn to the **preparation**, which I shall present as clearly as possible.

First of all, before the gold is joined with its water, it must be **calcined** <term>calcined</term> <note>The process of heating a metal to high temperatures until it is reduced to a fine, brittle powder or ash.</note> most subtly, so much so that its parts are even smaller than dust motes in the sun. For otherwise, it would resist the **solution** <term>solution</term> <note>The chemical process of dissolving a solid substance into a liquid medium to begin the transmutation.</note> and the work would become very tedious or even be ruined.

# The Epistle of Sir **GEORGE RIPLEY**
## To King Edward
### explained
### By **ANONYMOUS PHILALETHA** <note>A pseudonym for Eirenaeus Philalethes, likely the American-born alchemist George Starkey.</note>.

This Epistle, as it was addressed to a King <note>Likely King Edward IV of England (reigned 1461–1483), to whom Ripley dedicated several works.</note> who was both wise and courageous in his lineage; so too does it contain within it the entire secret, which is taught, described, and skillfully handled therein, in such a way—as the author himself testifies—that in this Epistle he unties various knots <note>metaphor for solving difficult alchemical riddles or procedural problems</note>. I can and do testify along with him that nothing is left to be desired <note>original: *desideriren*; meaning nothing is missing or lacking</note> here concerning the truth of the <term>Magisterium</term> <note>the "Mastery," the completed process of the "Great Work" to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>, both in its theory and its practice, which has not been fully taught in this short Epistle. This I now intend to add as an appendix, or rather as a key to all that I have

I have written an explanation of the tangled or confused sayings of this profound author <note>original: *Authoris*; referring to George Ripley</note>; that is, concerning his *Vision*, *Twelve Gates*, *Recapitulation*, *Admonition* or Remembrance, and his *Retam* <note>original: *Retam*; likely referring to Ripley's "Rithmi" or certain verses, often associated with the marrow or core of the work</note>, upon which I have commented extensively in a paraphrased or interpretative manner. And although I have written about these matters in various places with much and very great understanding, nevertheless, I do at times intermingle doubtful and apparently contradictory statements, affirming and saying "yes" to certain things which—if they were interpreted without figurative language—are outwardly false. This was done only to somewhat shroud, obscure, and hide the <term>Art</term> <note>the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy, traditionally kept secret from the "unworthy"</note>.

Here, however, I promise and assure you upon my word that I will no longer present or put forward doubtful or mystical thoughts, but will explain with a philosophical sincerity <note>original: *Philosophischen Auffrichtigkeit*</note>, in such a way and manner that a <term>Son of the Art</term> <note>a traditional term for an initiated student or practitioner of alchemy</note> shall have neither the need nor the desire for a more complete disclosure.

...for your sake, [I] admonish you that you should reciprocate our sincerity in this free communication <note>original: *communication*; referring here to the disclosure of alchemical secrets</note> by keeping this secret for yourselves, and in no way sharing it with anyone, unless it be a reliable friend on whom you can depend not to make it common <note>meaning to publicize the secret to the uninitiated or "unworthy" masses</note>.

Therefore, in explaining this epistle <note>the specific text or letter by George Ripley that Philalethes is currently analyzing</note>, I will not rely on the method of teaching that I used in the previous commentary <note>original: *Commentario*</note>, which was written in a paraphrased <note>original: *paraphrasticè*; an interpretative style that expands on the original wording</note> or interpretative manner; rather, for the explanation of the theory, I will first present the philosophy that lies within this epistle under various conclusions or logical arguments <note>original: *Schlußreden*</note>, and then interpret and clarify them. ...[which] shall be introduced through aphorisms <note>original: *Aphorismos*; short, pithy statements of truth or principle</note> or brief maxims, according to the true understanding and intention of this philosopher <note>original: *Philosophi*; referring here to George Ripley, the author of the epistle</note>, in this his indeed brief, yet vigorous <note>original: *nervosen*; here meaning dense, forceful, or full of "nerve"</note> and very profound epistle. To this we wish to add our own opinion and explanation, as is fitting for a faithful and truthful commentator to append to his text.

### The First Conclusion.

The first conclusion or logical argument <note>original: *Schlußrede*</note> is based on the 9th proposition (the first eight propositions are mostly complimentary or decorative rhetoric) and is briefly this: namely, that all things can be multiplied within their own kind and genus, and thus also the metals, which possess within them the capacity for the imperfect to be transformed into the perfect <note>this refers to the alchemical belief in transmutation, where "base" metals like lead are evolved into "noble" metals like gold</note>.

II. The second is based on the tenth proposition, namely: that the very foundation regarding the possibility of transformation is the possibility of the reduction <term>reduction|In alchemy, this refers to the process of returning a solid metal to its "first matter" or a liquid state so it can be reworked</term> of all metals and such minerals that are metalli-

<summary>Philalethes begins his formal analysis of George Ripley's work, presenting the first two "conclusions" of alchemical theory: that metals can be multiplied in nature and that transmutation is possible through the reduction of metals to their primary state.</summary>
<keywords>aphorisms, George Ripley, transmutation, multiplication of metals, alchemical reduction, metallic genus</keywords>

--- Page 293 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the list of alchemical "conclusions" derived from George Ripley's epistle. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the reduction of metals and minerals into their primary components.</meta>

metallic principles <note>original: *Metallische Principia*</note> into their first Mercurial matter <note>original: *Mercurialische Materia*; in alchemy, this is the "First Matter" from which all metals are thought to originate, often described as a metallic water or liquid</note>.

III. The third is grounded in the eleventh proposition, and is this: that among so many various metallic and mineral sulfurs <note>original: *Schwefeln*; the active, combustible, or "masculine" principle in alchemy</note>, there are only two which serve our Work; these sulfurs have their Mercuries <note>original: *Mercurios*; the passive, fluid, or "feminine" principle</note> essentially and substantially united with them.

IV. The fourth, taken from the eleventh proposition, is that he who understands these two sulfurs and Mercuries will find that one is entirely pure and red, and the other white, which very few are able to rightly understand or find <note>red and white represent the two stages or "tinctures" of the Great Work: the white stone which turns metals to silver, and the red stone which turns them to gold</note>.

V. The fifth conclusion from the twelfth proposition is that if a man’s principles are true and right, and the decoction <term>decoction|The process of steady heating or "cooking" a substance in a closed vessel to bring it to maturity</term> or cooking <note>original: *Kochung*</note> of these same principles or beginnings is correctly arranged according to the Art, then its outcome and final result will also be certain and right, namely to know the true Magisterium <term>Magisterium|The "Mastery" or the completed Great Work; the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone</term>.

These conclusions are indeed few in number and brief in concept, yet in importance and substance or value they are priceless; and therefore the expansion, explanation, and interpretation of the same
